Like I still have a crew waiting on me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still when you're not doing one of those little jobs around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I suppose. I mean, I can sit if I have to, I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything. But like in church, or if there’s a quiet talk or meeting, I notice I’m shifting around, tapping my foot, or feeling like I need to stand up and stretch. I know I’m supposed to stay put, so I try to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not all the time, but enough that I notice it. If I’m trying to paint, or even just plan out a little story in my head, and I hear a truck go by outside—or I suddenly think of something I meant to do in the garden—my mind just kind of jumps over there. Then I lose where I was and have to back up. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t mean to be rude. In the storytelling meetings, sometimes I’ll answer or jump in before the other person has quite finished. Then right after, I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. A bit, yes… though I hope not in a big dramatic way. My wife’s very patient, but she’s kind of had to take over keeping the calendar, and she checks behind me on things because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then… well, it doesn’t always happen the way it should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess since I stopped working, I’ve had a hard time just… settling down. It’s not terrible or anything, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’m kind of up and moving all day, going from one little job to the next around the house. Like I still have a crew waiting on me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still when you're not doing one of those little jobs around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I suppose. I mean, I can sit if I have to, I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything. But like in church, or if there’s a quiet talk or meeting, I notice I’m shifting around, tapping my foot, or feeling like I need to stand up and stretch. I know I’m supposed to stay put, so I try to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not all the time, but enough that I notice it. If I’m trying to paint, or even just plan out a little story in my head, and I hear a truck go by outside—or I suddenly think of something I meant to do in the garden—my mind just kind of jumps over there. Then I lose where I was and have to back up. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t mean to be rude. In the storytelling meetings, sometimes I’ll answer or jump in before the other person has quite finished. Then right after, I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsivity affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… though I hope not in a big dramatic way. My wife’s very patient, but she’s kind of had to take over keeping the calendar, and she checks behind me on things because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then… well, it doesn’t always happen the way it should.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize more than once at the community center and with the garden group for letting people down. I don’t like saying that, because I really do mean well. But yes, I guess it has affected people around me some.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overly angry or irritable for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not for no reason. I mean, I can get a little frustrated now and then, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m often angry or irritable out of the blue. My wife might notice things better than I do, but from my side, no, that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that about myself. I might talk a bit if it’s a topic I’m comfortable with, but I don’t think I’m usually the one dominating the conversation. I hope not, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least some. I was always told I was a bright kid, which was nice, but the teachers would also say I was kind of scattered. I’d lose pencils all the time, call out answers when I probably should’ve waited, and I needed reminders to finish my work before recess.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but yes, looking back, there were signs like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess since I stopped working, I’ve had a hard time just… settling down. It’s not terrible or anything, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’m kind of up and moving all day, going from one little job to the next around the house. Like I still have a crew waiting on me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still when you're not doing one of those little jobs around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I suppose. I mean, I can sit if I have to, I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything. But like in church, or if there’s a quiet talk or meeting, I notice I’m shifting around, tapping my foot, or feeling like I need to stand up and stretch. I know I’m supposed to stay put, so I try to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not all the time, but enough that I notice it. If I’m trying to paint, or even just plan out a little story in my head, and I hear a truck go by outside—or I suddenly think of something I meant to do in the garden—my mind just kind of jumps over there. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t mean to be rude. In the storytelling meetings, sometimes I’ll answer or jump in before the other person has quite finished. Then right after, I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsivity affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… though I hope not in a big dramatic way. My wife’s very patient, but she’s kind of had to take over keeping the calendar, and she checks behind me on things because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then… well, it doesn’t always happen the way it should.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize more than once at the community center and with the garden group for letting people down. I don’t like saying that, because I really do mean well. But yes, I guess it has affected people around me some.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overly angry or irritable for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not for no reason. I mean, I can get a little frustrated now and then, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m often angry or irritable out of the blue. My wife might notice things better than I do, but from my side, no, that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that about myself. I might talk a bit if it’s a topic I’m comfortable with, but I don’t think I’m usually the one dominating the conversation. I hope not, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least some. I was always told I was a bright kid, which was nice, but the teachers would also say I was kind of scattered. I’d lose pencils all the time, call out answers when I probably should’ve waited, and I needed reminders to finish my work before recess.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but yes, looking back, there were signs like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I remember some of it from when I was younger, like I said, but as far as saying it’s been consistent my whole life… I don’t know if I could say that confidently.\n\nThere were probably periods where things were easier or I managed better, so I may not have noticed it as much. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties, such as trouble sitting still or focusing, significantly interfered with your ability to work or get along with others?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it’s interfered some, yes. I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing deadlines at a job or anything, but at home my wife has pretty much taken over the calendar and kind of checks behind me, just to make sure I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nAnd socially, or with groups I’m in, I’ve had to apologize more than once to the community center and the garden group for letting people down. I don’t like saying that, because I try to be dependable, but… yes, it has caused some problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently since you were a child, without any long periods where they weren't a problem?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “without any long periods,” honestly. I don’t remember it clearly enough to be that definite.\n\nI know there were things when I was a kid, and I know some things still come up now, but whether it was a steady problem the whole way through… I’d hate to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really am. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess since I stopped working, I’ve had a hard time just… settling down. It’s not terrible or anything, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’m kind of up and moving all day, going from one little job to the next around the house. Like I still have a crew waiting on me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still when you're not doing one of those little jobs around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I suppose. I mean, I can sit if I have to, I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything. But like in church, or if there’s a quiet talk or meeting, I notice I’m shifting around, tapping my foot, or feeling like I need to stand up and stretch. I know I’m supposed to stay put, so I try to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not all the time, but enough that I notice it. If I’m trying to paint, or even just plan out a little story in my head, and I hear a truck go by outside—or I suddenly think of something I meant to do in the garden—my mind just kind of jumps over there. Then I lose where I was and have to back up. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t mean to be rude. In the storytelling meetings, sometimes I’ll answer or jump in before the other person has quite finished. Then right after, I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsivity affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… though I hope not in a big dramatic way. My wife’s very patient, but she’s kind of had to take over keeping the calendar, and she checks behind me on things because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then… well, it doesn’t always happen the way it should.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize more than once at the community center and with the garden group for letting people down. I don’t like saying that, because I really do mean well. But yes, I guess it has affected people around me some.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overly angry or irritable for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not for no reason. I mean, I can get a little frustrated now and then, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m often angry or irritable out of the blue. My wife might notice things better than I do, but from my side, no, that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that about myself. I might talk a bit if it’s a topic I’m comfortable with, but I don’t think I’m usually the one dominating the conversation. I hope not, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least some. I was always told I was a bright kid, which was nice, but the teachers would also say I was kind of scattered. I’d lose pencils all the time, call out answers when I probably should’ve waited, and I needed reminders to finish my work before recess.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but yes, looking back, there were signs like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I remember some of it from when I was younger, like I said, but as far as saying it’s been consistent my whole life… I don’t know if I could say that confidently.\n\nThere were probably periods where things were easier or I managed better, so I may not have noticed it as much. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties, such as trouble sitting still or focusing, significantly interfered with your ability to work or get along with others?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it’s interfered some, yes. I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing deadlines at a job or anything, but at home my wife has pretty much taken over the calendar and kind of checks behind me, just to make sure I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nAnd socially, or with groups I’m in, I’ve had to apologize more than once to the community center and the garden group for letting people down. I don’t like saying that, because I try to be dependable, but… yes, it has caused some problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently since you were a child, without any long periods where they weren't a problem?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “without any long periods,” honestly. I don’t remember it clearly enough to be that definite.\n\nI know there were things when I was a kid, and I know some things still come up now, but whether it was a steady problem the whole way through… I’d hate to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really am. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties in multiple settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in other activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least in more than one place. At home, my wife has kind of taken over keeping the calendar and checking behind me, just so things don’t get missed.\n\nAnd outside the house, with things like the community center and the garden group, I’ve had to apologize more than once for not following through or letting people down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, it does show up in different settings.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had significant difficulties with behavior or attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they did, though I don’t want to overstate it. Back in grade school, teachers would say I was bright, but kind of scattered.\n\nI remember things like losing pencils a lot, calling out answers when I probably should’ve waited, and needing reminders to finish my work before going out to recess. It wasn’t like I was trying to cause trouble, at least I don’t think so… but yes, that was mentioned. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or impulsivity in most of the places you've been, like at home, in social situations, and in other activities, for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything. Even back in grade school, my teachers would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nI was always losing pencils, and I’d sometimes call out answers instead of waiting my turn. I also remember needing reminders to finish my work before recess. So… yes, it does go back pretty far, at least as far as I can remember from school. Does that fit what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or impulsivity at work, when you were working?\n\nPatient: It’s been a while since I was working, so I’m not sure I can give you a really clear answer there.\n\nI don’t want to say yes just to make it fit if I can’t remember specific examples. I think it probably did carry into those years some, yes… though the clearest memories I have are still from grade school.

Even back in grade school, my teachers would say I was bright, just scattered. I was always losing pencils, calling out answers, needing reminders to finish my work before recess. So I think it’s been there since I was a kid, just maybe in different amounts. Is that what you’re asking?

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with paying attention or behaving than your friends when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little more than some of my friends, yes. I don’t want to overstate it, because I wasn’t trying to be a problem kid or anything. But even in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but scattered.\n\nI was the one losing pencils, calling out answers sometimes, and needing reminders to finish my work before recess. So… compared to other kids, I guess it probably stood out some. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Were these difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or impulsivity something you struggled with during your teenage years as well?\n\nPatient: I think it probably did carry into those years some, yes… though the clearest memories I have are still from grade school.\n\nBack then, teachers would say I was bright but scattered, and I was always losing pencils or calling out answers, and needing reminders to finish my work before recess. So by the time I was a teenager, I don’t think it just disappeared, but I don’t want to claim more than I clearly remember. Is that fair?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or impulsivity consistently since you were a child, without any periods where they significantly improved?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, yes… not like every single day was awful or anything, but I don’t remember a time where it really went away.\n\nEven back in grade school, my teachers would say I was bright, just scattered. I was always losing pencils, calling out answers, needing reminders to finish my work before recess. So I think it’s been there since I was a kid, just maybe in different amounts. Is that what you’re asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in your life where these difficulties, such as trouble sitting still or focusing, significantly improved or seemed to go away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure I can point to a time when it clearly went away. I mean, there were probably times things felt a little easier, or I managed better, but I wouldn’t say there was some long stretch where I thought, “Oh, that’s not an issue anymore.”

At least, not that I can remember clearly. Does that make sense?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess since I stopped working, I’ve had a hard time just… settling down. It’s not terrible or anything, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’m kind of up and moving all day, going from one little job to the next around the house. Like I still have a crew waiting on me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sit still when you're not doing one of those little jobs around the house?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I suppose. I mean, I can sit if I have to, I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything. But like in church, or if there’s a quiet talk or meeting, I notice I’m shifting around, tapping my foot, or feeling like I need to stand up and stretch. I know I’m supposed to stay put, so I try to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not all the time, but enough that I notice it. If I’m trying to paint, or even just plan out a little story in my head, and I hear a truck go by outside—or I suddenly think of something I meant to do in the garden—my mind just kind of jumps over there. Then I lose where I was and have to back up. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t mean to be rude. In the storytelling meetings, sometimes I’ll answer or jump in before the other person has quite finished. Then right after, I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsivity affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… though I hope not in a big dramatic way. My wife’s very patient, but she’s kind of had to take over keeping the calendar, and she checks behind me on things because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then… well, it doesn’t always happen the way it should.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize more than once at the community center and with the garden group for letting people down. I don’t like saying that, because I really do mean well. But yes, I guess it has affected people around me some.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overly angry or irritable for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not for no reason. I mean, I can get a little frustrated now and then, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m often angry or irritable out of the blue. My wife might notice things better than I do, but from my side, no, that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… at least I haven’t really noticed that about myself. I might talk a bit if it’s a topic I’m comfortable with, but I don’t think I’m usually the one dominating the conversation. I hope not, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least some. I was always told I was a bright kid, which was nice, but the teachers would also say I was kind of scattered. I’d lose pencils all the time, call out answers when I probably should’ve waited, and I needed reminders to finish my work before recess.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but yes, looking back, there were signs like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties, like trouble sitting still or focusing, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I remember some of it from when I was younger, like I said, but as far as saying it’s been consistent my whole life… I don’t know if I could say that confidently.\n\nThere were probably periods where things were easier or I managed better, so I may not have noticed it as much. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties, such as trouble sitting still or focusing, significantly interfered with your ability to work or get along with others?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it’s interfered some, yes. I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing deadlines at a job or anything, but at home my wife has pretty much taken over the calendar and kind of checks behind me, just to make sure I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nAnd socially, or with groups I’m in, I’ve had to apologize more than once to the community center and the garden group for letting people down. I don’t like saying that, because I try to be dependable, but… yes, it has caused some problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently since you were a child, without any long periods where they weren't a problem?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “without any long periods,” honestly. I don’t remember it clearly enough to be that definite.\n\nI know there were things when I was a kid, and I know some things still come up now, but whether it was a steady problem the whole way through… I’d hate to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really am. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties in multiple settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in other activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least in more than one place. At home, my wife has kind of taken over keeping the calendar and checking behind me, just so things don’t get missed.\n\nAnd outside the house, with things like the community center and the garden group, I’ve had to apologize more than once for not following through or letting people down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, it does show up in different settings.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had significant difficulties with behavior or attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they did, though I don’t want to overstate it. Back in grade school, teachers would say I was bright, but kind of scattered.\n\nI remember things like losing pencils a lot, calling out answers when I probably should’ve waited, and needing reminders to finish my work before going out to recess. It wasn’t like I was trying to cause trouble, at least I don’t think so… but yes, that was mentioned. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or impulsivity in most of the places you've been, like at home, in social situations, and in other activities, for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything. Even back in grade school, my teachers would say I was bright, but scattered.\n\nI was always losing pencils, and I’d sometimes call out answers instead of waiting my turn. I also remember needing reminders to finish my work before recess. So… yes, it does go back pretty far, at least as far as I can remember from school. Does that fit what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or impulsivity at work, when you were working?\n\nPatient: It’s been a while since I was working, so I’m not sure I can give you a really clear answer there.\n\nI don’t want to say yes just to make it fit if I can’t remember specific examples. I’m sure I had normal frustrations like anyone, but I don’t have a strong memory of it being a big issue at work. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with sitting still, focusing, or impulsivity in most of the places you've been, consistently over the years?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least from what I can remember. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but even back in grade school my teachers would say I was bright, just kind of scattered.\n\nI was always misplacing pencils, blurting out answers sometimes, and they’d have to remind me to finish my work before recess. So it does seem like it was there over the years, in that way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with paying attention or behaving than your friends when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little more than some of my friends, yes. But even in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but scattered.\n\nI was the one losing pencils, calling out answers sometimes, and needing reminders to finish my work before recess.
